> AGENT

Good question to stop on, because "spike" and "walking skeleton" aren't two options here — they're the **same first slice** seen from two angles:

- **Spike** = the *activity*: a time-boxed investigation with a decision at the end (eng-universal Rule C5).
- **Walking skeleton** = the *deliverable* it produces: one real initiative pushed end-to-end through every layer — deliverable → capability → story → task — as the actual markdown file-set (Rule B2).

For *this* initiative they collapse into one piece of work, and it's unambiguously first, for two reasons:

1. **It's the only work that can run while the project is parked.** Everything downstream waits on the `initiative-shape` Linear-decouple. The spike doesn't — it feeds goal+KRs in *as text* (by hand), which is exactly why we made KR2 gradable without the decouple. So it's parked-safe.
2. **It's the kill gate.** Our kill condition fires precisely here: if the file-set can't *walk deterministically* and *convert mechanically*, the standalone-markdown premise is wrong and we stop. Riskiest assumption, first slice. That's the point of it.

**The thing I'd push back on:** detailing the *downstream* issues right now. The spike's job is to settle **A (new skill) vs B (expand `planning-and-task-breakdown`)** — and that fork reshapes issues 3–8. Writing acceptance criteria for "build the skill" before we know whether it's a skill or an expansion is planning a Later bet in detail — the exact anti-pattern our own Not-Doing list names. So I'd keep the spike concrete and the rest coarse.

Here's the full ~8-issue shape against the three KRs (appetite was ~9):

**Spike — concrete, ready now (KR1, the gate):**
1. **Walking-skeleton spike: hand-produce the file-set + read off the contract.** Take one already-shaped initiative, build the full deliverable→capability→story→task markdown set by hand; read the schema into `docs/delivery-shape-contract.md` (layout · cross-ref convention · per-node tags). *Tests "expresses as walkable markdown" — first half of the kill condition.*
2. **Walk-script + conversion proof + A-vs-B decision `[GATE]`.** `bin/walk-delivery-plan` emits a tracker-shaped manifest; verify it maps mechanically to Linear milestone/issue/sub-issue. Then write the new-skill-vs-expand decision on that evidence. *Second half of the kill condition; the initiative proceeds, reshapes, or dies here.*

**Downstream — blocked on #2's decision + the decouple:**
3. Build it per A-vs-B — decomposition + foundational/skeleton prompt + Rule A1 design-doc branch + delegation up/down.
4. `bin/check-plan-framing` — grader: every story carries AC, ≥1 foundational task (KR2 read-side).
5. Self-trial on 2 initiatives → `examples/delivery-plans/_trials/` + note (KR2 grades here).
6. `eval/delivery-shape-triggers.md` — 8–10 phrasings (KR3 write-side).
7. `bin/eval-triggers` — zero collisions with initiative-shape / planning-and-task-breakdown (KR3 grades here).
8. `using-this-pack` flowchart — slot delivery-shape between the two (MVP discoverability).

44	---
45	
46	# Planning and task breakdown
47	
48	## Purpose
49	
50	This skill takes an accepted design doc and produces an ordered task list where each task is small enough to complete and verify independently. It bridges the gap between "we have an accepted design" and "we know exactly what to build in what order." The output is a sequence of well-scoped tasks, each with acceptance criteria, that whichever build skill the team uses will pick up one at a time.
51	
52	The goal is not comprehensiveness — it is clarity and verifiability. A task list that looks thorough but whose tasks are too big to verify will not survive contact with implementation. Every task must answer: "how do I know this is done?"
53	
54	## When to use
55	
56	- A design doc has been accepted and implementation is ready to begin.
57	- A well-scoped spec exists for a change that doesn't meet the Rule A1 design-doc trigger, but is complex enough that sequencing and acceptance criteria are non-obvious.
58	
59	## When not to use
60	
61	- **No accepted design doc and the work exceeds a Rule A1 trigger** — run `design-doc` first. A draft or in-flight doc does not count as accepted.
62	- **The scope is unclear or contested** — resolve scope ambiguity in the design doc before breaking down tasks. Task breakdown from a vague spec produces tasks nobody believes in.
63	
64	## Inputs
65	
66	An accepted design doc covering at minimum: the problem being solved, the chosen approach, the components being changed or created, and the constraints. The design doc does not need to be perfect; it needs to be stable enough that the approach won't change during implementation.
67	
68	## Outputs
69	
70	An ordered task list at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`. Each task has: a one-sentence description, acceptance criteria (how you know it's done), and any upstream dependencies called out. The list begins with the walking skeleton as task 1.
71	
72	## Workflow
73	
74	**1. Read the design doc end-to-end.**
75	Do not start decomposing until you have read the full document. The most common task breakdown error is starting with the first section and ignoring the constraints and operability sections at the end. Those sections often constrain the sequencing.
76	
77	**2. Identify the walking skeleton.**
78	The walking skeleton is the thinnest possible slice that exercises the full path end-to-end — from input to output — without filling in the real functionality. It is always task 1. If you cannot identify a walking skeleton (a slice that makes the system run, however crudely), the design doc is missing something. Stop and resolve it before proceeding.
79	
80	Examples:
81	- API endpoint: a handler that accepts the request, returns a hardcoded response, passes a smoke test — no real logic yet.
82	- UI feature: a route that renders the page with static content — no data binding yet.
83	- Pipeline stage: a step that accepts the input format and emits the output format with placeholder data — no processing yet.
84	
85	**3. Identify the feature slices.**
86	Starting from the walking skeleton, list the increments that build toward the full design. Each increment should:
87	- Extend functionality in one direction (add real logic, add a field, add an error path)
88	- Be independently testable
89	- Leave the system in a working, deployable state
90	
91	Do not batch unrelated concerns into one slice. If a task touches the data layer AND the API AND the UI, it is three tasks.
92	
93	**4. [GATE] Write acceptance criteria for each task.**
94	Before ordering tasks, write the "done" check for each one. An acceptance criterion is a test, an assertion, or a measurable condition — not a description of what to build. If you cannot write the acceptance criterion, the task is not well enough understood to schedule.
95	
96	Format: `Task is done when: [specific, verifiable condition]`
97	
98	- Bad: "Task is done when the search feature is implemented."
99	- Good: "Task is done when: a query against the `/search` endpoint returns a 200 with the correct result shape for a known test input, covered by an automated integration test."
100	
101	**5. [GATE] Size check.**
102	Review every task. Any task that:
103	- Cannot be described in one sentence
104	- Has more than one acceptance criterion that requires a separate deployment to verify
105	- Cannot be completed and verified in a single uninterrupted implementation session — i.e. it bundles multiple independently verifiable changes (see `eng-principles-agentic.md` P8: size by slice count and verification granularity, not hours)
106	
107	is too big. Split it. Size is not about effort estimation; it is about verification granularity. Small tasks surface problems early.
108	
109	Example — too big:
110	> "Implement user authentication, including sign-up, login, password reset, and session management."
111	
112	Split into verifiable tasks:
113	> Task A: POST /auth/signup creates a user and returns a JWT — covered by an integration test.
114	> Task B: POST /auth/login returns a JWT for valid credentials, 401 for invalid — covered by tests.
115	> Task C: POST /auth/password-reset sends a reset email — covered by a test mocking the mailer.
116	> Task D: Session expiry revokes access on subsequent requests — covered by a test advancing time.
117	
118	Each task has one observable outcome, one test, and one commit boundary. That is a correctly sized task.
119	
120	**6. Order by dependency.**
121	Sequence tasks so that each one's dependencies are complete before it starts. Call out explicit dependencies (on other tasks in this list, on external work, on platform capabilities). If a task depends on work from another team, flag it immediately — Rule C4: dependencies are surfaced before commitment.
122	
123	**7. Write and file the task list.**
124	Use the template below. File at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
125	
126	## Task list template
127	
128	```markdown
129	# Tasks: <feature-slug>
130	
131	Design doc: docs/designs/<feature-slug>.md
132	Last updated: <date>
133	
134	## Task list
135	
136	### Task 1 — Walking skeleton
137	**Description:** [one sentence — what does the skeleton do?]
138	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
139	**Dependencies:** none
140	
141	### Task 2 — [name]
142	**Description:** [one sentence]
143	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
144	**Dependencies:** Task 1
145	
146	### Task N — [name]
147	**Description:** [one sentence]
148	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
149	**Dependencies:** [list, or "none"]
150	
151	## Open questions
152	<!-- Anything blocking or uncertain that was surfaced during breakdown.
153	     Do not start implementation until these are resolved. -->
154	```
155	
156	## Common rationalisations
157	
158	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
159	|---|---|
160	| "The tasks are obvious from the design doc — no need to write them out." | If they're obvious, writing them out takes 15 minutes. If it takes more, they weren't as obvious as assumed. The acceptance criteria are worth the time even if the tasks aren't. |
161	| "We'll figure out the order as we go." | Unordered tasks produce surprise dependencies mid-implementation. Dependency discovery during a build is expensive; discovery before the build is cheap. |
162	| "This task is a bit big but we know what we're doing." | Task size is not a trust issue — it is a verification issue. Big tasks fail silently. Small tasks fail loudly and early. |
163	| "The design doc isn't fully accepted but it's close enough." | An almost-accepted design doc means the approach is still negotiable. Tasks broken down from a negotiable design are likely to be redone. |
164	| "We don't need a walking skeleton — we'll integrate at the end." | Integration at the end is the most expensive integration there is. The walking skeleton exists to find integration problems on day 1, not day 29. |
165	| "We already know the dependencies — no need to list them." | Dependencies change during implementation; listing them before forces you to verify each one rather than discover them when they block you. A listed dependency that turns out not to exist costs nothing. An unlisted dependency that surfaces mid-sprint costs days. |
166	| "I'll write acceptance criteria after I implement so I know what 'done' looks like." | By then you've already built the wrong thing. Acceptance criteria written before implementation are a spec — they constrain what you build. Criteria written after implementation describe what you built. The goal is the former. |
167	| "The task list will need to change anyway, so there's no point spending time on it." | The value is not the list surviving implementation intact. It is the thinking done while creating it — discovering the dependencies, sizing the slices, identifying the walking skeleton. A plan that gets revised is still better than no plan. |
168	
169	## Red flags
170	
171	- The walking skeleton is missing or is listed as something other than task 1.
172	- Any task has more than one independent acceptance criterion.
173	- Acceptance criteria say "feature is implemented" or "code is written" rather than describing a verifiable condition.
174	- Acceptance criteria reference implementation details ("the function is written") rather than observable behaviour ("the endpoint returns X for input Y").
175	- The task list was written before reading the constraints section of the design doc.
176	- A task depends on unresolved external work that hasn't been flagged.
177	- The list was generated from the design doc introduction without reading the full document.
178	- A "setup" or "scaffolding" task appears before the walking skeleton, deferring integration discovery.
179	
180	## Verification / exit criteria
181	
182	The skill has run correctly when:
183	
184	1. A task list exists at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
185	2. Task 1 is the walking skeleton, with an acceptance criterion that can be checked independently of downstream tasks.
186	3. Every task has a single, concrete acceptance criterion stating a verifiable condition.
187	4. Every task can be described in one sentence.
188	5. Dependencies are listed for every task that has them.
189	6. Any external or cross-team dependency has been surfaced explicitly in the open questions section.
190	7. No "setup" or "scaffolding" task precedes the walking skeleton — if infrastructure is needed before the skeleton, the skeleton task encompasses it.
191	8. The open questions section is either populated with owners and resolution dates, or explicitly marked empty because no unresolved questions remain.
192	
193	## References
194	
195	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — Rule A1 (design doc trigger), Rule B1 (YAGNI per slice), Rule B2 (walking skeleton first), Rule C4 (dependencies before commitment)
196	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P3 (no drift from design doc)
197	- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — the upstream output that this skill consumes
198	- Ryan Singer — "Shape Up" (appetite and scope as planning primitives)
199	
```

42	---
43	
44	# Initiative shape
45	
46	## Purpose
47	
48	initiative-shape is the entry point for creating a new initiative. It takes a vague idea — a sentence, a direction, a problem — and shapes it into a properly formed Linear project with a goal sentence (Objective), 3 measurable key results (each with baseline / target / measured over / how we'll know, a commit/stretch tag, and a bet/brake/foundation role), affected repos, bounded appetite, kill condition, and project type. The shaped initiative is then created in Linear.
49	
50	The skill exists because initiatives shaped without key results become repo-aliased backlogs, and backlogs without observable outcomes don't drive decisions. The Objective and KRs must be defined before the work begins — not inferred once the issues are closed (Rules P2, A3, C1, agentic Principle 3).
51	
52	## When to use
53	
54	- Starting any body of work that will span 5 or more issues.
55	- Converting an existing repo-project into a properly formed initiative.
56	- Preparing 3 initiatives for an upcoming cycle — run this once per initiative.
57	- When "I want to work on X" and X is clearly bigger than a single issue or bug fix.
58	
59	## When not to use
60	
61	- **Single-issue, bug, or KTLO work** — create the issue directly and put it in the ops slot. The ops slot has no goal/KR requirement.
62	- **Unvalidated ideas that haven't cleared idea-triage** — run `idea-triage` first if you're unsure the problem is worth pursuing at all.
63	- **Scoping an already-formed initiative** — use `planning-and-task-breakdown` once goal + key results are confirmed.
64	
65	## Inputs
66	
67	The vague idea in any form: a sentence, a project name, a direction, a problem statement fragment. The skill probes for everything else — do not require the user to pre-format anything.
68	
69	Optional: a list of existing open issues the user expects to belong to this initiative.
70	
71	## Outputs
72	
73	A Linear project (via `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_project`) whose description follows the six-field initiative format: Goal / Key results / Affected repos / Appetite / Kill condition / Project type. The description must read as a product OKR, not as pack documentation — field names are PM-readable and the rubric vocabulary (Layer 1 dimension, Layer 2 template) is confined to an italic audit-footer, never the KR body. Each KR's headline is the observable claim and carries a `(commit|stretch)` grading tag plus a `bet`/`brake`/`foundation` role tag; below it sit four sub-fields (baseline, target, measured over, how we'll know) and the audit-footer line. The project starts in Planned state — it does not enter a cycle until cycle planning.
74	
75	## Workflow
76	
77	**1. Capture the raw idea.**
78	Write it down verbatim. Do not reframe it yet.
79	
80	**2. [GATE] Problem or solution?**
81	Read the raw idea. Is it framed as something to build ("add X", "integrate Y") or a problem to solve ("users can't Z", "the model output isn't usable")? If solution, probe: "What goes wrong if we don't build this?" If the underlying problem can't be articulated, the initiative is not ready. Return for clarification; do not proceed.
82	
83	**2.5. Probe — project type.**
84	Before asking outcome questions, capture which of the six project types this initiative belongs to. The type sets the Objective shape and the rubric the KRs are judged against — a methodology skill pack and a personal product can both pass cycle close, but they pass on different KRs, because their theories of success are different.
85	
86	Ask:
87	
88	> "Which type? `1` methodology skill pack · `2` personal product · `3` utility skill pack · `4` research/thesis · `5` equity research · `6` production / customer-facing."
89	
90	One-paragraph descriptions of each type are in `references/initiative-types.md` — load it if the user wants the longer form. If the user can't pick a single type, the initiative likely contains two outcomes — flag and split before proceeding.
91	
92	**3. Probe — outcome questions.**
93	
94	**Load the type-specific playbook first.** Open `references/initiative-types.md` and read the Objective shape, default KR mix, and anti-patterns for the type chosen at Step 2.5. The KR mix names which leading + lagging dimensions this initiative is scored along — propose KRs from those dimensions rather than from a blank page.
95	
96	Default KR mix by type:
97	
98	- **Type 1 (methodology skill pack)** — invocation-rate KR (leading) + decision-quality KR (lagging) + anti-output guard KR (committed). See playbook for a worked example on `/initiative-shape` itself.
99	- **Type 2 (personal product, single-user)** — correctness KR (lagging) + no-silent-failure KR (committed) + maintenance-burden KR (committed). Availability KR optional 4th when scheduled-job completion is the central guarantee. Growth, MAU, retention KRs are categorically inapplicable — there is one user.
100	- **Type 3 (utility skill pack)** — first-shot correctness KR (aspirational, lagging) + coverage KR (committed) + use-log discipline KR (committed). Domain-specific quality KR (pricing accuracy, print acceptance, sale outcome) optional 4th when the pack has a quantifiable downstream signal. Use-log discipline is non-negotiable — every invocation must be logged at the moment of use to a named location (e.g. `listings/`, `outputs/`, `runs/`) with input, generated artefact, final-posted artefact, and edit-or-not outcome, or the lagging first-shot correctness KR is ungradable. Time-to-result KRs are usually vanity for Type 3 — only include if speed is the named bottleneck.
101	- **Type 4 (research / thesis-driven)** — knowledge-claim KR (committed) + critique-survivability KR (aspirational, lagging) + source-discipline KR (committed). Adoption KR (paying audience engagement, validated buyer interest) is a later-stage 4th — defer until critique-survivability has held. Readership of an undefended thesis is noise; "publish N essays" or "N readers" KRs are output, not defensibility. See Type 4 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`.
102	- **Type 5 (equity research tooling)** — pre-registration KR (committed) + calibration KR (aspirational, lagging) + hit-rate KR (aspirational, lagging). Decision-quality KR (per-call postmortem) optional 4th when the cycle window covers prior 12-month outcomes. Pre-registration is non-negotiable — every BUY/WATCH/AVOID call must be written to a dated, immutable artefact (cached `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` + git history) before outcomes are visible, or the lagging KRs are ungradable. This is the Tetlock standard: calibration over a portfolio of pre-registered calls beats accuracy on any single name.
103	- **Type 6 (production / customer-facing)** — activation/retention/conversion KR (aspirational, lagging) + quality-pair KR (committed — NPS, p95 latency, error rate, or support volume per user) + telemetry-discipline KR (committed). Growth without a paired quality counterweight is the canonical failure mode — every value KR must have a paired quality KR. Telemetry must be in place before the cycle starts (no telemetry = no Type 6 KR); "ship feature X to 100% of users" and un-paired MAU/DAU KRs are Cagan's canonical output-disguised-as-outcome. See Type 6 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`.
104	
105	Then ask explicitly. Do not infer. Wait for a response before synthesising.
106	
107	- **Who is affected?** Which users, operators, or contexts does this problem touch?
108	- **What's the negative outcome if this isn't solved?** What task fails, what decision can't be made, what workflow breaks?
109	- **Which KR is the bet, which are brakes, which is foundation?** Before drafting observable states, name the split. The **bet** is what you're actually pushing forward this cycle — its target is a delta or a new state. **Brakes** are things that already work and you only want to protect from regression in pursuit of the bet — target is "stay ≥ baseline" or "stay at 0". **Foundation** is the instrumentation, telemetry, or tracking that has to exist before the bet KR is even measurable. Most initiatives have at most 1–2 bets; if everything feels like a bet, the brakes haven't been named yet. (Roles defined in `references/kr-quality-templates.md` "Roles within an initiative".)
110	- **What 3 observable states would tell you it worked?** (Cap at 5; default 3 — per Wodtke.) Each one should be something a future agent can verify by looking at the system — a binary pass/fail, a fitness function firing, or a measurable delta. Avoid "improve X" / "better Y" language and avoid arbitrary "run N times" thresholds. **For all six types, propose the default mix from the playbook (Type 1: invocation-rate + decision-quality + anti-output guard; Type 2: correctness + no-silent-failure + maintenance-burden, with availability as an optional 4th; Type 3: first-shot correctness + coverage + use-log discipline, with domain-specific quality as an optional 4th — and never omit the use-log discipline KR for Type 3, and require the use-log location be named as a file path or directory in the KR's how-we'll-know field; Type 4: knowledge-claim + critique-survivability + source-discipline, with adoption as a later-stage 4th — defer adoption KRs until critique-survivability has held; Type 5: pre-registration + calibration + hit-rate, with decision-quality postmortem as an optional 4th — and never omit the pre-registration KR for Type 5; Type 6: activation/retention/conversion + quality-pair + telemetry-discipline — every value KR must have a paired quality KR, and telemetry must be in place before the cycle starts) and ask the user to confirm or reshape — do not start from a blank page when a playbook applies.**
111	- **For each KR, fill all four sub-fields (PM-readable labels — these are what appear in the Linear description):**
112	  - **baseline** — current value or state (if unknown, the first issue in the initiative is to measure it)
113	  - **target** — value or state we expect at the end
114	  - **measured over** — when this is judged ("by end of cycle", "across next 4 cycles", "within 30 days")
115	  - **how we'll know** — where the evidence will live (a file path, a Linear query, a log, a cached report; if grader-backed, the named file plus the one-sentence command that emits the verdict)
116	  - And tag each KR `(commit)` (committed — must hit 1.0: operability, no-silent-failure, baseline tracking) or `(stretch)` (aspirational — 0.6–0.7 is success: outcome KRs, behaviour change, forecast calibration). The tags are PM-readable shorthand; the Wodtke grading-bar semantics are unchanged. A mixed OKR with 1–2 `(commit)` + 1–2 `(stretch)` is the common shape.
117	  - And tag each KR with its **role** — `bet` / `brake` / `foundation` (from the probe above), appended in italics to the KR headline (e.g. `*(bet)*`). The role is orthogonal to `(commit|stretch)`: a brake can be either, depending on how strict its target is.
118	- **Which repos does this touch?** Name them. Cross-repo scope is allowed; name it explicitly.
119	
120	**4. Probe — scope and kill condition.**
121	Two separate questions:
122	
123	- **Appetite — how many issues?** Guide: 5 ≈ small (1–2 days), 10 ≈ medium (full cycle slot), 15 ≈ large (fills the whole cycle). If the answer exceeds 15, the initiative needs splitting — flag this now.
124	- **Kill condition — when do we stop?** Name the observable state that says "the bet didn't work, walk away." An initiative with KRs but no kill condition becomes a zombie. Phrasing: "If [KR] fails for [N] consecutive cycles" / "If we ship [X] and [baseline metric] doesn't move" / "If we learn [Y] in research".
125	
126	**5. Synthesise into initiative format.**
127	Draft the six fields (OKR-shaped — Goal is the Objective; Key results are 3 observable claims with full sub-field discipline). Use the template in the next section — write it so it reads as a product OKR: PM-readable field names, rubric vocabulary confined to the audit-footer.
128	
129	Each KR's claim (its headline) should be one of: a binary pass/fail ("X works on the common path with no manual intervention"), a fitness function firing ("the guard fails the run loudly when Y"), or a measurable delta ("token footprint drops vs baseline"). No "improve X" / "better Y" / "run N times" language. Every KR must have all four sub-fields (baseline / target / measured over / how we'll know), a `(commit|stretch)` tag, and a `bet`/`brake`/`foundation` role appended to the headline. Add the audit-footer line (`*Layer 1: … · Layer 2: …*`) under each KR, and the dimensions-summary line after the KR list — this is the rubric trace, kept out of the KR body so the OKR reads cleanly to a non-pack-author.
130	
131	Present the draft. Do not create the Linear project yet.
132	
133	**6. [GATE] User confirms the draft.**
134	Ask explicitly: "Does this capture the initiative correctly? Any changes to the Objective, the Key results, kill condition, or project type before I create the project?" Do not proceed until confirmed. Fixing a wrong problem statement, vague KR, or missing kill condition here takes one minute; fixing it mid-cycle costs days.
135	
136	**6.5. [GATE] Verification rubric — 11 cross-cutting rules.**
137	Run the following checks against the confirmed draft. Each rule is a binary pass/fail. Any failure returns the draft to Step 5 for repair — the Linear project is NOT created until all 11 pass.
138	
139	| # | Rule | Pass condition |
140	|---|---|---|
141	| 1 | All six fields present | Goal / Key results / Affected repos / Appetite / Kill condition / Project type are all non-empty |
142	| 2 | Goal names who + outcome | Sentence contains an affected party and a desired outcome — no solution verb ("build", "add", "integrate", "ship") |
143	| 3 | Initiative name is a goal/problem | Name does not open with a solution verb ("Build X", "Add Y", "Integrate Z") |
144	| 4 | At least 3 KRs, no more than 5 | KR count ≥ 3 and ≤ 5 |
145	| 5a | No degenerate KRs | `target ≠ baseline` either numerically (different number) or by state change (different observable state) — bans "baseline 100%, target 100%" patterns |
146	| 5b | KRs name distinct Layer 1 dimensions | Across all KRs, no two share the same Layer 1 dimension. Default vocabulary: correctness / outcome / maintenance / discipline (see `references/kr-quality-templates.md`). An out-of-default dimension (e.g. performance) is permitted only when the initiative genuinely has that property |
147	| 5c | KRs are not Goal-restatements | Strip the Goal to its measurable noun-phrase; no KR's observable state matches it. Each KR must name a *measurable property of* the Goal — not the Goal itself |
148	| 5d | KR matches its Layer 2 template | For each KR, name which Layer 1 dimension it sits in and confirm it matches the template (golden-path test / self-trial protocol / structural cap + revisit gate / artefact-exists + sections-complete) in `references/kr-quality-templates.md` |
149	| 5e | Language ban | No KR contains "improve", "better", "enhance", or arbitrary "run N times" count thresholds without a correctness or quality dimension |
150	| 5f | KR is grader-backed (or carved out with justification) | Each KR's `how we'll know:` field points at a command / script / query that emits a verdict against the target — describable in one sentence at draft time. Manual grading is permitted only under the carve-out: (a) the initiative is genuinely one-shot or throwaway, OR (b) the verdict is irreducibly qualitative ("the prose reads naturally", "the diagram is legible") and no honest automatable proxy exists. The carve-out must be stated alongside the KR. Building the grader (write-side schema + read-side command) is scoped into the initiative as one or two user stories before it enters Active. See `references/kr-quality-templates.md` "The grader-backed KR pattern" |
151	| 6 | Every KR has all four sub-fields, no placeholders, `how we'll know` is a concrete artefact | Each KR has baseline, target, measured over, how we'll know — none blank, none equal to `TBD`, `unknown`, `currently unknown`, `we'll measure it`, `we'll figure it out`, `various`, `the logs`, `the system`, `the dashboard`, `better than today`, `the report`. `how we'll know` points to a concrete artefact: a file path, a directory, a Linear query, a log query (e.g. `bigquery: events WHERE ...`), a cached report URL, or a metric name + system |
152	| 7 | Every KR is tagged | Each KR carries exactly one of `(commit)` or `(stretch)` |
153	| 8 | Kill condition is present | Non-empty; names an observable state, not a vague clause ("if it doesn't work") |
154	| 9 | Project type is canonical | One of: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, or 6 — not free text |
155	| 10 | Appetite is in issues | Expressed as "~N issues" — not days, weeks, or sprints |
156	| 11 | Every KR has a role tag | Each KR carries exactly one of `bet` / `brake` / `foundation` — roles defined in `references/kr-quality-templates.md` "Roles within an initiative" |
157	
158	**Soft check (warning, not a hard fail):** if every KR is tagged `bet`, surface a warning — most initiatives have at most 1–2 bets, and an all-bet draft usually means the brakes and foundation were silently omitted. This does not block project creation, but name it to the user and ask whether a brake (something that already works and shouldn't regress) or foundation (instrumentation the bet depends on to be measurable) KR is missing. Do not force one of each role — some shapes are honestly 2 bets + 1 foundation, or 1 bet + 2 brakes.
159	
160	Report each failing rule by number with the specific text that triggered the failure. For rule 5, name the specific sub-rule (5a / 5b / 5c / 5d / 5e / 5f) that failed, so the user knows whether to fix the baseline-equals-target problem (5a), the duplicate-dimension problem (5b), the Goal-restatement problem (5c), the template-mismatch problem (5d), the language problem (5e), or the missing-grader problem (5f). When all checks pass, proceed to Step 7.
161	
162	**7. Create the Linear project.**
163	Call `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_project` with:
164	- `name`: goal or problem label — not a solution name, not a repo name
165	- `description`: the six-field initiative format (see template below)
166	- Status: Planned
167	
168	Confirm the project URL and share it.
169	
170	**8. Optional: assign known issues.**
171	If the user listed existing issues for this initiative, list them and offer to reassign them to the new project via `mcp__claude_ai_Linear__save_issue`. Assign only the ones the user confirms.
172	
173	## Initiative description template
174	
175	The output is the Linear project description. It must read as a product OKR — a non-pack-author should be able to grade each KR in 30 seconds. Each KR's headline *is* the observable claim (there is no separate `state:` field). The four sub-fields use PM-readable names; the rubric vocabulary lives only in the italic audit-footer under each KR and the dimensions-summary line after the list — never in the KR body. The headline ends with a PM-readable `*(bet|brake|foundation)*` role tag (the push / a guardrail / an enabler) — plain English, so it stays on the headline, unlike the Layer 1/Layer 2 rubric vocabulary that lives in the footer.
176	
177	```markdown
178	**Goal:** For [who], we want to [solve problem / achieve outcome].
179	
180	**Key results:**
181	
182	**KR1 (commit|stretch)** — [the observable claim as one readable sentence: binary pass/fail, fitness function firing, or measurable delta] *(bet|brake|foundation)*
183	- baseline: [current value/state]
184	- target:   [target value/state]
185	- measured over: [time frame or N units — "by cycle close", "next 10 listings", "within 30 days"]
186	- how we'll know: [where the evidence lives — file path / query / log / cached report; if grader-backed, the named file plus the one-sentence command that emits the verdict]
187	
188	*Layer 1: [dimension] · Layer 2: [template]*
189	
190	**KR2 (commit|stretch)** — [observable claim] *(bet|brake|foundation)*
191	- baseline: ...
192	- target:   ...
193	- measured over: ...
194	- how we'll know: ...
195	
196	*Layer 1: [dimension] · Layer 2: [template]*
197	
198	**KR3 (commit|stretch)** — [observable claim] *(bet|brake|foundation)*
199	- baseline: ...
200	- target:   ...
201	- measured over: ...
202	- how we'll know: ...
203	
204	*Layer 1: [dimension] · Layer 2: [template]*
205	
206	*Dimensions: [d1] / [d2] / [d3] — all distinct per rule 5b.*
207	
208	(3 KRs default; cap at 5. When the key results hold — or are definitively ruled out — the initiative is Done. Each headline ends with a role — `bet` (the push), `brake` (don't regress), or `foundation` (makes the bet measurable); most initiatives have at most 1–2 bets.)
209	
210	**Affected repos:** [list]
211	
212	**Appetite:** ~[N] issues
213	
214	**Kill condition:** [the observable state that says "stop pursuing this Objective"]
215	
216	**Project type:** [1: methodology | 2: personal product | 3: utility skill pack | 4: research/thesis | 5: equity research | 6: production]
217	```
218	
219	The `*Layer 1 · Layer 2*` footer and the `*Dimensions: …*` summary line are the rubric trace (they back the Step 6.5 checks 5b and 5d). They sit *below* the KR body, set in italics, so the OKR itself reads cleanly. Do not put Layer/rule annotations inside a KR headline or sub-fields.
220	
221	## Common rationalisations
222	
223	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
224	|---|---|
225	| "I know what the goal is — I don't need to write it down." | The KRs aren't for you right now; they're for the agent in the next session who has no memory of this conversation. Write them down. |
226	| "The key results will be obvious once the work is done." | Defining them after the work is done is how "shipped = done" creeps in. KRs are what convert a list of closed issues into an achieved outcome. |
227	| "One KR is enough — the goal sentence covers the rest." | One KR collapses easily into a single arbitrary threshold. 3 KRs force you to name the dimensions that actually matter (correctness, no-blocking, no-silent-failure, speed) — and that's the discipline. |
228	| "I need a 3rd KR — let me add one for documentation / tests / logging." | If your 3rd KR's only reason to exist is to clear rule #4, you have two real dimensions and you're padding. A meta-KR that wasn't load-bearing pre-gate is filler — and rule 5b will catch it as a duplicate of "discipline" if you already have a discipline KR. Two honest paths: (a) **reshape** — a real third Layer 1 dimension is hiding (typically maintenance or outcome, see `references/kr-quality-templates.md`); (b) **descope** — narrow the initiative until 2 KRs are honestly sufficient and the work fits an ops slot rather than an initiative slot. Padding to clear the gate is the failure mode the gate exists to catch. |
229	| "I don't know the baseline — I'll add it later." | If baseline isn't known, the first issue in the initiative is to measure it. A KR without a baseline is an aspiration, not a result — you can't grade it at cycle close. |
230	| "The target is obvious from the goal — I don't need to spell it out separately." | The target is what makes the KR scoreable. Without it, "we'll know it when we see it" replaces a binary pass/fail, and the post-launch review degrades to vibes. |
231	| "I don't need a measured-over window — we'll just keep at it until it lands." | Without a measured-over window, a KR cannot be closed. Open-ended KRs become zombies — they neither succeed nor fail, they just live on the project page forever. |
232	| "How we'll know is obvious — it's just whether the feature works." | Name the artefact. A file path, a Linear query, a log line, a cached report. If you can't name where the evidence lives, the KR isn't inspectable — and an uninspectable KR is a feeling, not a result. |
233	| "Why a kill condition? We're going to make this work." | Most bets don't pay off on the first try. The kill condition is what protects the next quarter from a sunk-cost zombie. Naming it up front costs one sentence; not naming it costs an entire cycle. |
234	| "Project type is bureaucratic — we know what kind of thing this is." | The field exists so the next agent — or you in three weeks — can apply the right rubric without re-deriving the taxonomy. It's a one-token tag and a downstream skill needs it. |
235	| "We just need to author N new skills this cycle — the pack is the deliverable." | For methodology skill packs (Type 1), volume of skills authored is output, not outcome. Success is invocation accuracy at the decision moment + decision quality when invoked. Reshape into invocation-rate + decision-quality KRs — see Type 1 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
236	| "Improving skill quality this cycle is enough — we don't need a KR for it." | "Improve quality" fails Wodtke's weekly-trackable test. Reshape into a decision-quality KR: in ≥X of last N sampled invocations, the skill surfaced an issue or produced an artefact that would otherwise not have appeared. Now it has a sample, target, and window — and can be graded at cycle close. |
237	| "Let's add user registration so we can onboard people to [personal product]." | Type 2 is single-user by definition — no users to register. If signup is genuinely the work, the initiative is Type 6 (production / customer-facing), not Type 2 — re-classify before proceeding. |
238	| "Let's set a KR to increase MAU / DAU / retention on the personal app." | Categorically inapplicable to Type 2. There is one user. The dimensions that matter are correctness, no-silent-failure, and maintenance burden — growth KRs belong to Type 6. See Type 2 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
239	| "We just need to add more skills to the pack this cycle — wider coverage is the win." | For utility skill packs (Type 3), volume of skills is output, not outcome. Success is first-shot correctness of the artefact and coverage of the categories the user actually encounters end-to-end. Five more skills with no use-log produce zero graded improvement. Reshape into first-shot correctness + coverage KRs over the categories actually hit — see Type 3 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
240	| "Let's run `/resell-au` N times this cycle as a usage drive." | Activity vanity. Twenty invocations with bad outputs is failure, not progress. Type 3 grades on the quality of each artefact, not the count of them. Reshape into a first-shot correctness KR ("≥X of next N posted without text edits") over the invocations you would have made anyway. |
241	| "Skip the use-log — I'll remember which invocations went well." | Memory is selective recall of the wins. Without immutable per-invocation capture at the moment of use — input, generated artefact, final-posted artefact, edit-or-not outcome — the lagging first-shot correctness KR collapses into a vibe check. For Type 3, the use-log discipline KR is non-negotiable, parallel to Type 5's pre-registration KR. Name the use-log location as a file path or directory in the KR's source field. |
242	| "Let's run `/stock-screen` on a bunch of new tickers this cycle." | Type 5 grades on calibration, not throughput. Twenty new screens with no pre-registration produce twenty unrecorded opinions, not twenty results. Reshape into a pre-registration KR over the names you actually call — see Type 5 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
243	| "We'll just check that BUY calls outperform the S&P over the next year." | Un-pre-registered and cherry-pickable. Without a dated, immutable set of names locked at call time, "outperform" becomes whatever cherry-picked window the analyst chooses post-hoc. Reshape into a hit-rate KR over the explicit pre-registered cohort and add a pre-registration KR (committed) to lock the cohort. |
244	| "Skip the pre-registration KR for the equity initiative — we already write reports per call." | Writing reports is not pre-registration. Pre-registration means the call is dated, committed to git, and immutable before the 12-month window closes. Without the immutability check, the lagging calibration and hit-rate KRs are arguments about what was meant, not checks of what was claimed (Tetlock). For Type 5, the pre-registration KR is non-negotiable. |
245	| "We just need to publish N essays this cycle to ground the thesis." | For research/thesis-driven (Type 4), essay count is output, not defensibility. A thesis with 20 unsourced essays is weaker than one with 3 source-backed claims that hold under critique. Reshape into knowledge-claim + critique-survivability KRs — see Type 4 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
246	| "Let's set a KR for N readers / N newsletter subscribers on the thesis." | Adoption masquerading as outcome on an undefended thesis. Readership of a thesis that hasn't cleared expert critique is noise — it tells you the headline is catchy, not that the assertions are right. Defer adoption KRs until critique-survivability has held; otherwise the wrong audience signal entrenches a wrong thesis. |
247	| "Just ship feature X to 100% of users this cycle as the win." | Cagan's canonical output-disguised-as-outcome for Type 6. Rollout is implementation; the OKR scores whether the customer behaviour the feature was supposed to change actually moved. Reshape into a behaviour-change KR (activation/retention/conversion) with a paired quality KR — see Type 6 playbook in `references/initiative-types.md`. |
248	| "Let's set a KR to increase MAU / DAU." | Type 6 value KR without a paired quality KR is half a KR. MAU can be inflated by re-engagement emails that crater NPS, or by registration prompts that bloat the denominator. Every growth KR is paired with a quality counterweight (NPS, retention, churn, support-per-user) — the pair is what makes the vanity-gain trade visible. |
249	| "This is too big for 15 issues but it's one coherent thing." | Split by outcome: which key result do you want first? That's one initiative. The rest follow. |
250	| "I'll sort out the KRs after we create the project." | Step 6 is a gate. The project doesn't get created until the KRs (with all sub-fields) and kill condition are confirmed. |
251	| "The affected repo is obvious — we only work in one repo here." | Name it anyway. The field exists for cross-repo legibility, not to teach you something you don't know. |
252	
253	## Red flags
254	
255	- A KR says "improve X" or "better Y" with no observable state to verify.
256	- KRs are arbitrary "run N times" thresholds rather than common-path correctness, no-silent-failure, or measurable-delta states.
257	- Only 1 or 2 KRs — the initiative is probably under-specified along the dimensions that actually matter (default 3; cap 5).
258	- A KR is missing one or more of baseline / target / measured over / how we'll know.
259	- A KR is missing its `(commit)` or `(stretch)` tag — the rubric for grading it at cycle close is undefined.
260	- A KR is missing its `bet` / `brake` / `foundation` role tag — the initiative hasn't said whether the KR is a push, a guardrail, or an enabler (rubric rule 11).
261	- Every KR is framed as a forward push (no `brake`, no `foundation`) — usually a sign the initiative hasn't named what the actual bet is vs what's load-bearing as a guardrail. Reshape: identify which 1–2 KRs are the bet, mark the rest as brakes or foundation.
262	- No kill condition — the initiative has no defined off-ramp and will become a zombie when the bet doesn't pay off.
263	- The Project type field is missing or set to a free-text label that doesn't match the six-type taxonomy.
264	- For Type 1 (methodology skill pack), every KR scores artefact volume (skills authored, lines written, files edited) — no invocation-rate (leading) or decision-quality (lagging) KR. The OKR is then measuring output, not outcome.
265	- For Type 2 (personal product, single-user), any KR targets growth metrics (MAU, DAU, retention, signups) or feature volume — the type was misclassified, or growth metrics were imported from a product model that doesn't apply.
266	- For Type 3 (utility skill pack), every KR scores skill volume ("N skills authored") or invocation count ("run N times this cycle") — the OKR is measuring output, not utility. Reshape into first-shot correctness (≤ one edit before posting/printing) and coverage (zero unhandled categories) KRs.
267	- For Type 3, no use-log discipline KR (committed) and no how-we'll-know location named as a file path or directory for the first-shot correctness KR — the lagging KR is ungradable because there's no immutable per-invocation record to grade against.
268	- For Type 5 (equity research tooling), no pre-registration KR (committed) — every BUY/WATCH/AVOID call must be logged to a dated, immutable artefact (cached `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` + git history) before outcomes are visible, or the lagging calibration and hit-rate KRs are ungradable by construction.
269	- For Type 5, KRs target throughput ("run `/stock-screen` on N tickers", "produce N reports") or un-pre-registered outperformance ("BUY calls beat the S&P") — Type 5 grades on calibration of dated, locked cohorts, not on volume of analyses or post-hoc cherry-picked windows.
270	- For Type 4 (research / thesis-driven), every KR scores essay count, reader count, or newsletter subscribers — no knowledge-claim or source-discipline KR. The OKR is measuring artefact volume or audience noise, not defensibility of the assertions.
271	- For Type 6 (production / customer-facing), a growth / conversion / retention KR has no paired quality KR — Cagan's canonical failure mode where the value KR is achieved by destroying NPS, latency, error rate, or support volume per user.
272	- For Type 6, a KR uses feature-rollout language ("ship X to 100%", "launch Y") rather than naming the customer behaviour the feature was supposed to change — the OKR is measuring delivery, not outcome.
273	- For Type 6, telemetry isn't in place before the cycle starts and there is no telemetry-discipline KR to instrument it — the value and quality KRs cannot be graded because the baseline can't be read and definitions can drift mid-cycle.
274	- The initiative name describes a solution ("Build the X feature") rather than a goal or problem.
275	- The appetite is expressed as a time duration ("2 weeks") rather than an issue count.
276	- The Linear project was created before Step 6 confirmed the draft.
277	- An initiative with > 15 issues in appetite was created without a split decision.
278	
279	## Verification / exit criteria
280	
281	The skill has run correctly when:
282	
283	1. A Linear project exists with a description containing all six canonical fields (Goal / Key results / Affected repos / Appetite / Kill condition / Project type).

I've read both skills end-to-end. Before I open up the idea space, let me restate the problem and surface the tension I found — because it changes what "refine" even means here.

## Restate (How Might We)

**How might we make "plan a project" a single top-down motion — goal → initiative → deliverables → verifiable tasks — that bakes in your preferred framing and never silently skips foundational work?**

## The tension I need to flag before we go further

Your mental model is **goals ↔ projects → project deliverables → tasks**, framed around user stories and capabilities. But the existing `planning-and-task-breakdown` skill is built on a *different* philosophy, and the two collide in three concrete places:

1. **Input mismatch.** The skill consumes an *accepted design doc* (`docs/designs/<slug>.md`) — not a Linear initiative. Yet `initiative-shape` explicitly names `planning-and-task-breakdown` as its downstream. There's no actual seam between them. Your workflow (initiative → tasks) jumps a step the skill assumes exists.

2. **Framing mismatch.** The skill is deliberately *anti*-user-story. It organises work around **walking-skeleton-first vertical slices** sized by verification granularity ("one sentence, one acceptance criterion, one commit"). A "capability" or "user story" is usually too big to be one slice, and the walking skeleton deliberately cuts *across* stories. So the framing you keep hand-prompting is the one the skill was designed to avoid.

3. **Foundational work is actively forbidden.** Red flag #8 and exit criterion #7 say: *no "setup" or "scaffolding" task may precede the walking skeleton* — fold it into the skeleton instead. That's the opposite of "plan in the mise/toolchain setup explicitly." Your problem #2 is, structurally, a rule the skill enforces against you.

None of this means the skill is wrong — its engineering instincts are good. It means your top-down/capability mental model and the skill's slice-based execution model are two different layers that haven't been reconciled. The interesting design question is whether to **merge them, stack them, or bridge them.**

Let me get four things straight before I converge on a direction.

Locked in. Here's where that lands us:

- **Orchestrator** that runs the chain top-down and delegates to the existing skills (doesn't re-implement them).
- **All four layers** in your mental model: deliverables → capabilities → user stories → tasks.
- **Foundational work folded into the walking skeleton**, but surfaced by an explicit prompt so it's never silent.
- **Hybrid output**: walking skeleton stays task 1; verifiable slices grouped under capability/deliverable headers.

Before I write the one-pager, two honest pushbacks and the assumptions that could sink this.

## Stress-test

**This is the most valuable connective tissue the pack is missing — but it has two real failure modes.**

**Failure mode 1 — ceremony.** You picked all four layers. Materialising deliverable → capability → user story → task as four *distinct objects* for every project is overhead the pack's own principles (YAGNI, "code is a liability", smallest-solution) would reject. For a 5-issue initiative it's absurd. The fix is to make most layers **organisational headings, not separate artefacts**, and to collapse them when the initiative is small. My recommendation:

| Your layer | Where it actually lives | Counts against appetite? |
|---|---|---|
| Deliverable / milestone | Linear **milestone** on the project (maps 1:1 to a KR where possible) | No |
| Capability / epic | A **heading** in the task doc (and optionally a label) — not its own object | No |
| User story | Linear **issue** | **Yes** (this is the unit the ~N-issue appetite counts) |
| Task / slice | Sub-issue *or* a line under the story in `docs/tasks/<slug>.md`; walking skeleton first | No |

This keeps the appetite cap honest (stories = issues, ≤15) while still giving you the full hierarchy to *think* in.

**Failure mode 2 — the orchestrator silently re-implements its sub-skills, then drifts.** The whole pack rests on "memory lives in artefacts" (agentic P7). If `plan-project` copies initiative-shape's 11-rule rubric or planning-and-task-breakdown's gates into its own prose, those copies rot the moment the originals change. The orchestrator must be **thin** — a conductor that *invokes* the existing skills at each step and contributes only the one thing neither does today: the deliverable/capability decomposition between them.

## Hidden assumptions (these are what could kill it)

1. **Invocation, not logic, is the bottleneck.** Your stated pain is that you *forget* to prompt the framing. An orchestrator only fixes that if its description reliably triggers on "plan a project" / "I'm starting X." If discovery fails, you're back to manual. → This is a Type-1 (methodology) skill, so its leading success metric is *invocation rate at the right moment*, exactly per initiative-shape's own playbook.
2. **Skills can be chained from within a skill.** The realistic mechanism is a workflow doc that instructs the agent to run `initiative-shape`, then the decomposition, then `planning-and-task-breakdown` in sequence — not a literal function call. Needs confirming that this delegation reads cleanly.
3. **Linear is the home for deliverables/stories**, not local markdown. Milestones + issues is the natural fit and kills your manual seam — but it's the heaviest integration and the part most likely to need iteration.
4. **The design-doc step is conditional.** `planning-and-task-breakdown` *requires* an accepted design doc. So the chain must branch: Rule A1 trigger met → design-doc is a mandatory link; sub-A1 → initiative + deliverables suffice as the breakdown input.

## One-pager

# plan-project — top-down project planning orchestrator

## Problem Statement
**How might we make "plan a project" a single top-down motion — goal → initiative → deliverables → verifiable tasks — that bakes in the deliverable/capability/story framing by default and never silently skips foundational work?**

Today the pack has strong point skills (`initiative-shape`, `planning-and-task-breakdown`) but no executable connective tissue between them. The user re-prompts the framing every time, gets tasks that are too low-level or missing acceptance criteria, and forgets to plan foundational/toolchain work — because `planning-and-task-breakdown` consumes a design doc (not an initiative), is deliberately anti-user-story, and actively forbids pre-skeleton setup tasks.

## Recommended Direction
A **new thin orchestrator skill** (working name `plan-project`) that conducts the existing chain top-down and contributes the one missing step: decomposing a shaped initiative's KRs into deliverables → capabilities → user stories, before handing each capability to the existing slice engine.

Flow:
1. **Goal/initiative** → delegate to `initiative-shape` (creates the Linear project, goal + KRs). Skip if an initiative already exists.
2. **Rule A1 gate** → if the work meets a design-doc trigger, delegate to `design-doc`; else proceed on initiative + deliverables.
3. **Deliverable/capability decomposition** (the new contribution) → map each KR to a deliverable (Linear milestone), group work into capability headings, draft user stories (Linear issues) with acceptance criteria by default.
4. **Foundational prompt** → explicitly ask "what toolchain/setup must exist for the skeleton to run?" and *fold the answer into the walking skeleton task* (stack-agnostic — no tool names in prose, per pack rule).
5. **Task breakdown** → delegate to `planning-and-task-breakdown` per capability: walking skeleton as task 1, verifiable slices grouped under capability headers.

The orchestrator stays thin: it invokes, it doesn't re-implement. The hierarchy materialises as Linear milestones (deliverables) + issues (stories) + headings (capabilities) + slices (sub-issues/task-doc lines).

## Key Assumptions to Validate
- [ ] **Invocation reliability** — does the description trigger on "plan a project" / "let's plan this out" without colliding with `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s "break this down" triggers? *Test: write both descriptions, run a triggering eval on 8–10 phrasings.*
- [ ] **Chaining mechanism** — can a skill instruct the agent to run other skills in sequence and have it read cleanly? *Test: draft the delegation steps; confirm against an existing multi-skill flow in the pack.*
- [ ] **Layer→Linear mapping holds** — do deliverables-as-milestones and stories-as-issues survive the ~15-issue appetite cap? *Test: dry-run the mapping on one real past initiative; count resulting issues.*
- [ ] **Design-doc branch** — is the Rule A1 gate the right fork, and does sub-A1 breakdown-from-initiative satisfy `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s input contract? *Test: trace both branches against that skill's "When to use".*

## MVP Scope
**In:** the orchestrator SKILL.md (conductor + deliverable/capability decomposition + foundational prompt); the layer→Linear mapping table; the Rule A1 branch; delegation steps to the three existing skills. A small refinement to `planning-and-task-breakdown` so it accepts "initiative + deliverables" as a valid input alongside "design doc."

**Out:** rewriting `initiative-shape` or `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s internal logic; automating Linear milestone/issue creation beyond what the existing MCP tools already do; a greenfield foundational *checklist* (you chose fold-into-skeleton, so it's a prompt, not a checklist).

## Not Doing (and Why)
- **A greenfield setup checklist with named tools** — you chose fold-into-skeleton; and pack rules forbid stack-prescriptive prose (no "mise" in the skill text). It stays a prompt you answer.
- **Capability/epic as its own Linear object** — ceremony. It's a heading, not an artefact, until you have evidence you need to track it separately.
- **Replacing the walking-skeleton-first rule** — you chose hybrid, so the engineering rigour stays; capabilities organise *around* it, they don't displace it.
- **Copying sub-skill gates into the orchestrator** — guarantees drift. Delegate instead.

## Open Questions
- **Name.** `plan-project` (trigger-clear) vs. a `-shape`-family name. I lean `plan-project` to avoid clashing with `initiative-shape` (where "project" already = initiative). Your call.
- **Capability layer** — confirm it stays a heading/label and never a Linear object, or name the case where it should be one.
- **Where the deliverable/story artefacts live** — Linear milestones+issues (recommended) vs. an extended `docs/tasks/<slug>.md` that mirrors the hierarchy vs. both.
- **Does this also update `using-this-pack`'s lifecycle flowchart** so the orchestrator is discoverable from the meta-skill?

# plan-project — top-down project planning orchestrator (tool-agnostic)

## Problem Statement
**How might we make "plan a project" a single top-down motion — goal → deliverables → capabilities → stories → verifiable tasks — that bakes in the framing by default, never silently skips foundational work, and emits a standalone markdown plan that any tracker-layer (e.g. `agent-skills-workflow` → Linear) can consume?**

## Recommended Direction
A **new thin orchestrator skill** (working name `plan-project`) that takes a goal + KRs as text input and conducts the standalone planning chain, contributing the one missing step — decomposing the goal into deliverables → capabilities → user stories — and emitting **one markdown plan artefact**. It makes no tracker calls.

Flow:
1. **Input: goal + KRs** (pasted, or lifted from an existing initiative/Linear project — source-agnostic). No shaping *write* here.
2. **Rule A1 gate** → if the work meets a design-doc trigger, delegate to `design-doc` (markdown); else proceed on goal + deliverables.
3. **Deliverable/capability/story decomposition** (the new contribution) → deliverables map to KRs; capabilities are headings; user stories carry acceptance criteria by default.
4. **Foundational prompt** → explicitly ask "what toolchain/setup must exist for the skeleton to run?" and fold the answer into the walking-skeleton task (stack-agnostic — no tool names in prose, per pack rule).
5. **Task breakdown** → delegate to `planning-and-task-breakdown` per capability: walking skeleton as task 1, verifiable slices grouped under capability headers.

The orchestrator stays thin — it invokes `design-doc` and `planning-and-task-breakdown`, and does not re-implement their gates (avoids drift, agentic P7).

## The layers (standalone, all markdown)

| Your layer | Where it lives | Tracker adapter maps it to |
|---|---|---|
| Deliverable / milestone | Top-level section in the plan doc, tagged with the KR it serves | Linear milestone (in `agent-skills-workflow`) |
| Capability / epic | Heading under its deliverable | Label / parent issue — adapter's call |
| User story | Subsection with acceptance criteria | Linear issue |
| Task / slice | Sub-list under the story; walking skeleton first | Sub-issue / task line |

The right-hand column is **not our concern** — it documents the seam so the adapter author knows what to expect.

## Key Assumptions to Validate
- [ ] **Plan-artefact schema is a stable contract.** Its structure (heading levels, KR tags, story/AC format) must be predictable enough for `agent-skills-workflow` to parse and map. *Test: write the schema; have the adapter author (you) confirm it's mappable to milestones+issues without ambiguity.*
- [ ] **Invocation reliability** — triggers on "plan a project" / "let's plan this out" without colliding with `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s "break this down" triggers. *Test: triggering eval on 8–10 phrasings.*
- [ ] **Chaining reads cleanly** — a skill instructing the agent to run `design-doc` then `planning-and-task-breakdown` in sequence is legible, not a literal call. *Test: draft the delegation steps against an existing multi-skill flow.*
- [ ] **Design-doc branch** — the Rule A1 gate is the right fork, and sub-A1 breakdown-from-(goal+deliverables) satisfies `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s input contract. *Test: trace both branches against that skill's "When to use".*

## MVP Scope
**In:** the `plan-project` SKILL.md (conductor + deliverable/capability/story decomposition + foundational prompt); the **plan-artefact schema** (the contract, documented as a template — likely `docs/plans/<slug>.md`); the Rule A1 branch; a small refinement to `planning-and-task-breakdown` so it accepts "goal + deliverables" as a valid input alongside "design doc."

**Out:** any tracker writes (Linear or otherwise) — that's `agent-skills-workflow`; rewriting `initiative-shape`; extracting a Linear-free OKR-shaping core (separate follow-on).

## Not Doing (and Why)
- **Any Linear/tracker coupling** — the seam is the markdown plan artefact. Keeps this work standalone and the pack tool-agnostic.
- **Delegating to `initiative-shape`** — it ends in a Linear write; calling it re-couples us. Take goal + KRs as input instead.
- **Capability/epic as its own artefact** — it's a heading until there's evidence it needs separate tracking.
- **Replacing walking-skeleton-first** — hybrid keeps the rigour; capabilities organise around it.
- **Copying sub-skill gates into the orchestrator** — guarantees drift; delegate.

## Open Questions
- **The contract.** What exactly must the plan artefact guarantee structurally so `agent-skills-workflow` can map it deterministically? This is the highest-leverage design decision — worth its own short spec.
- **One doc or two.** Does `plan-project` emit a single `docs/plans/<slug>.md` holding the full hierarchy, or a plan doc that *links* to `planning-and-task-breakdown`'s `docs/tasks/<slug>.md`? Leaning single doc to avoid fragmentation.
- **Goal shaping inside the flow?** Take goal+KRs as pure input (current assumption), or include Linear-free shaping discipline lifted from `initiative-shape`?
- **Name** — `plan-project` (lead) vs. a `-shape`-family name.
- **Discoverability** — update `using-this-pack`'s lifecycle flowchart so the orchestrator is findable.

Good — these three lock down a lot. Let me take your direct question first, because it's the one that reshapes the rest.

## "Do shape-initiative and shape-project overlap?"

**Functionally, no — they're two different decisions made at two different confidence stages.** Naming-wise, yes, and that's the real problem.

- `initiative-shape` produces **the bet**: goal, KRs, appetite, kill condition, project type. You make it when confidence may still be low. Per Now-Next-Later, *most* initiatives sit in Next/Later and never get planned in detail — they might get killed first.
- The new skill produces **the plan**: deliverables → capabilities → stories → tasks. You only make it for an initiative you're committing to **Now**.

Merging them would force you to detail-plan every bet at definition time — which directly violates the pack's own principle (certainty decays with horizon; detail only the Now column). So **don't merge.** The bet and the plan are correctly separate layers, gated by commitment.

What you're actually sensing as "overlap" is two real things:

1. **The name.** In this workspace `linear-workflow.md` hard-codes *"Projects = initiatives."* So `project-shape` literally collides with `initiative-shape` — both would "shape the project." That's a genuine discovery hazard, not pedantry. My recommendation: stay in the `-shape` family but name it for *the plan*, not *the project* → **`delivery-shape`** (shapes how a committed initiative gets delivered). `project-shape` works only if you're willing to overload "project" against your own Linear rule. Your call, but I'd switch.

2. **A sharper question hiding underneath: is this a new skill at all, or an upward *expansion* of `planning-and-task-breakdown`?** Its unique contribution (deliverables→capabilities→stories) is thin; the leaf breakdown already exists. So:
   - **(A)** New orchestrator skill that delegates down to `planning-and-task-breakdown`.
   - **(B)** Expand `planning-and-task-breakdown` to accept an initiative, add the upper layers, keep one skill.

   I won't force this now — it's reversible and cheap to settle with evidence. **The walking-skeleton spike you proposed is exactly what decides it:** once you've hand-built the hierarchical markdown file-set for one real initiative, you'll see whether the upper layers carry enough unique structure to justify a separate skill (A) or fold cleanly into the existing one (B).

## Decisions integrated

- **Keep delegating to `initiative-shape`** — with an explicit dependency: *its Linear-decoupling must land first.* This project won't run until then, and that's fine; you're sequencing the decouple next.
- **Walking-skeleton spike is the first slice** — and it's a schema spike: a set of cross-referencing markdown files in hierarchical order, for one real initiative. The Linear conversion falls out of that structure, so proving the structure *is* proving the contract.
- **Name** → `delivery-shape` (recommended) over `project-shape` (collision).
- **Discoverability** → in scope (update `using-this-pack`'s flowchart).

# delivery-shape — top-down delivery planning (tool-agnostic)

## Problem Statement
**How might we turn a committed initiative into a complete, ordered delivery plan — deliverables → capabilities → stories → verifiable tasks — as a set of hierarchically cross-referenced markdown files, with framing baked in and foundational work never silent, so any tracker-layer (`agent-skills-workflow` → Linear) can map it mechanically?**

## Recommended Direction
A `-shape`-family skill (recommended name **`delivery-shape`**) that takes a shaped initiative (goal + KRs) and emits **a set of cross-referencing markdown files** representing the full delivery hierarchy. It delegates up and down rather than re-implementing:

1. **Upstream:** `initiative-shape` provides goal + KRs (delegated; depends on `initiative-shape` being Linear-decoupled first).
2. **Rule A1 gate:** design-doc trigger met → delegate to `design-doc`; else proceed on goal + deliverables.
3. **New contribution:** decompose into deliverables (each tagged to a KR) → capabilities (headings) → user stories (with acceptance criteria by default).
4. **Foundational prompt:** ask "what toolchain/setup must exist for the skeleton to run?" → fold into the walking-skeleton task (stack-agnostic, no tool names in prose).
5. **Downstream:** delegate to `planning-and-task-breakdown` per capability → walking skeleton task 1, verifiable slices under capability headers.

**The seam to `agent-skills-workflow` is the file-set structure** (directory layout + cross-reference convention + per-node tags), not a tool API. Ports-and-adapters: shaper owns the plan artefact; the workflow repo owns the Linear adapter.

## The layers (standalone markdown file-set)

| Layer | Lives as | Adapter (other repo) maps to |
|---|---|---|
| Deliverable / milestone | A file (or top section), tagged with the KR it serves | Linear milestone |
| Capability / epic | Heading / sub-file under its deliverable | Label / parent issue |
| User story | Subsection with acceptance criteria | Linear issue |
| Task / slice | Sub-list under the story; walking skeleton first | Sub-issue / task line |

## Walking-skeleton spike (first slice — do this before building the skill)
By hand, take **one real, already-shaped initiative** and produce the hierarchical markdown file-set end-to-end. Deliverables for the spike:
- A concrete file-set that a human can read top-down and a script could walk.
- The **schema/contract** read off that example: directory layout, cross-reference convention (front-matter links? relative paths? a manifest file?), and the per-node tags (deliverable↔KR, story↔AC, task↔skeleton flag).
- A **decision: (A) new skill vs (B) expand `planning-and-task-breakdown`** — based on how much unique structure the upper layers actually carried.
- A sanity check that the file-set converts to Linear artefacts mechanically (you've said it should — the spike confirms).

## Key Assumptions to Validate
- [ ] **Hierarchy is faithfully expressible as cross-referenced markdown** and walks deterministically. *Spike proves or breaks this.*
- [ ] **The file-set is mechanically convertible to tracker artefacts.** *Trace the example against Linear milestone/issue/sub-issue shapes.*
- [ ] **Invocation reliability** — triggers on "plan a project" / "let's plan this out" without colliding with `planning-and-task-breakdown` or `initiative-shape`. *Triggering eval on 8–10 phrasings (and resolves the naming collision empirically).*
- [ ] **`initiative-shape` decoupling lands first** — hard dependency; this skill stays parked until then.

## MVP Scope
**In:** the walking-skeleton schema spike (above); the documented plan-artefact contract; the skill (or the `planning-and-task-breakdown` expansion, per spike outcome) with the deliverable/capability/story decomposition + foundational prompt + Rule A1 branch + delegation; **an update to `using-this-pack`'s flowchart** for discoverability.

**Out:** any tracker writes (that's `agent-skills-workflow`); the `initiative-shape` decoupling itself (separate, sequenced-first work).

## Not Doing (and Why)
- **Merging the bet and the plan into one skill** — violates Now-Next-Later; you'd force premature detail-planning of Later bets.
- **Any Linear/tracker coupling** — the seam is the markdown file-set.
- **Naming it `project-shape`** (recommended against) — collides with `initiative-shape` given Projects ≡ initiatives.
- **Deciding new-skill vs expand up front** — let the spike's evidence settle it.
- **Copying sub-skill gates into the orchestrator** — drift; delegate.

## Open Questions
- **Name:** `delivery-shape` (lead) — confirm or override.
- **Cross-reference convention** for the file-set — the spike's core output.
- **(A) new skill vs (B) expand `planning-and-task-breakdown`** — spike decides.

2. **Save target** — `docs/ideas/delivery-shape.md`?
